  agent step 1: content: Ligand 1 is my working molecule and it is the weaker scaffold: QED 0.634, SA 3.80, and notably zero aromatic rings. Subtracting the QED and rescaled-SA contributions leaves JNK3 at essentially zero for both ligands, so the reachable gain this episode is in SA and QED, and SA 3.80 is the single worst term on the board.

The liability is the isopropoxyethyl tail: atoms 0-5 contribute six rotatable bonds, LogP -1.70, and an uncommon 2-(isopropoxy)ethylsulfonyl motif that is what drives SA up. Replacing that whole branch with a phenyl converts the molecule into a benzenesulfonamide - an extremely common, readily synthesized motif that should pull SA down sharply, add the aromatic ring the molecule completely lacks, and cut rotatable bonds, all of which push QED up.

I am cutting the acyclic S-CH2 bond: anchor on the sulfur (idx 6, kept) and branch on idx 5, which removes the entire CCOC(C)C tail, then attaching phenyl in its place. I am not using crossover here - ligand 2's aromatics are attractive but its own QED is only 0.457, and a targeted substructure swap gives me a specific, predictable product rather than a recombination I cannot aim.

  agent step 2: content: That edit taught me something I had wrong. It did everything I predicted structurally - MW 285 to 239, rotatable bonds 4 to 2, LogP 2.95 to 2.48 - and QED did not move at all (0.7464 to 0.7455). The reason is that QED's molecular-weight term peaks around 300 Da, so at 285 I was already near optimum and trimming to 239 gave back in the mass term exactly what I gained in flexibility and lipophilicity. Trimming mass is only useful when a molecule is heavy.

So the correction is to add mass back, but as something polar and rigid rather than as the floppy thioether linker I just removed. Putting a morpholine on the benzoxazole benzo ring takes MW to about 325 - back over QED's optimum - while adding an H-bond acceptor and holding LogP near 2.2. Morpholine has been consistently good for both QED and SA on this run.

JNK3 is an ATP-competitive kinase and its best-characterised inhibitor classes are 2-aminopyrimidines, aminopyrazoles and indazoles - all of which work by presenting an aromatic ring nitrogen with an adjacent NH directly to the hinge, as a paired acceptor and donor. I expect to pay for this: MW falls to roughly 265 and QED likely drops toward 0.85, costing perhaps 0.1 on the terms I can see. That is a deliberate trade. QED and SA have maybe 0.05 left between them here, whereas JNK3 sits at zero with far more available, and this is the single most direct way to reach for it.

  agent step 1: content: QED 0.796 and SA 2.34 leave modest room, but this scaffold already carries the pairing I care about: an amide NH directly attached to an electron-poor heteroaromatic, here 4-aminopyridine, fused onto a benzoxazole-2-carboxamide. That is a hinge-binding arrangement, and it is worth strengthening rather than trading away.

The upgrade is to move from aminopyridine to 2-aminopyrimidine. In the pyridin-4-yl the ring nitrogen sits para to the NH, too far to chelate the hinge together with it; in a pyrimidin-2-yl the NH is flanked by ring nitrogens on both sides, giving the adjacent donor-acceptor pair that the 2-aminopyrimidine inhibitor class - one of the best-characterised for this kinase - actually uses.

The cost is close to nil: pyrimidin-2-yl and pyridin-4-yl differ by one carbon for one nitrogen, so MW stays near 325 and QED and SA should barely move. This is close to a free change in the observable terms in exchange for a materially better hinge geometry.

  agent step 1: content: QED 0.934 and SA 1.97 are near their limits on this scaffold, and JNK3 is zero, so I am spending this episode on JNK3 again - with a different chemotype from the aminopyrimidine I tried on this scaffold earlier, so the two bets stay distinguishable.

The pyrazole core here is already one recognised JNK3 chemotype, but it is wasted: the amide it carries leads to a glycine-morpholine tail that is flexible, aliphatic and contributes nothing to binding. The other well-documented class for this kinase is the indazoles, which the field reached by evolving pyrazole-ureas through to thiophene-indazoles.

Replacing the acetamide-morpholine with an indazole-5-carbonyl puts both chemotypes in one molecule: an aminopyrazole amide whose acyl group is an indazole, giving an indazole NH and ring nitrogen as a donor-acceptor pair alongside the existing amide NH. MW lands near 331 and I expect QED around 0.78, so roughly 0.15 of observable cost.

  agent step 1: content: Back on the benzoxazole scaffold, and I want a cheap edit this time - my last two JNK3 bets cost 0.16 and 0.35 of observable Phi, and after the indazole result I am wary of swapping in whole ring systems.

A single atom does the work here. Atom 23 is the benzoxazole ring oxygen; replacing it with nitrogen converts the benzoxazole into a benzimidazole. That matters because 2-aryl-benzimidazoles are a specifically documented potent and selective JNK3 class, and because it introduces an NH donor into the fused ring while leaving everything else - the carboxamide, the aminopyridine, the morpholine - untouched.

  agent step 2: content: The benzimidazole conversion failed on a kekulization error - turning a ring oxygen into a nitrogen requires that nitrogen to carry an explicit hydrogen, and the tool did not add one, so the aromatic system could not be resolved. That is the second aromatic-nitrogen operation this run that has been unavailable to me; an N-aryl to N-alkyl swap on a pyrazole failed earlier on a valence error. Both are routine medicinal chemistry.

Taking a different route to the same goal. The pyridine ring already contributes a hinge acceptor, but its NH donor sits para to the ring nitrogen - too far apart to engage the hinge as a pair. Adding an amino group at atom 5, which is ortho to the ring nitrogen at atom 6, creates a 2-aminopyridine: an NH2 donor immediately adjacent to a ring-nitrogen acceptor, which is the classic hinge-binding pairing and the same geometry the 2-aminopyrimidine class relies on.

Atom 5 is an aromatic CH with a free hydrogen, and this adds to carbon rather than nitrogen so it should avoid the failure mode above. Expected cost is small - MW to about 339 and one extra donor.

  agent step 1: content: Third product from this same parent, so again a distinct heteroaryl in place of the piperidine filler. The two I have already placed here were a pyrimidin-2-ylamine and a 2-aminopyrimidin-4-ylamine; this time I want a fused bicyclic hinge binder rather than a monocycle.

4-aminoquinazoline is the choice. Quinazolines are among the most thoroughly validated ATP-competitive kinase scaffolds - the anilinoquinazoline class is the basis of several marketed kinase inhibitors - and the 4-amino nitrogen sits adjacent to N3, giving the same donor-acceptor pair as an aminopyrimidine but with an additional fused ring occupying the hydrophobic pocket alongside the hinge.

The cost is mass: MW goes from 315 to roughly 375, past QED's optimum near 300, so I expect QED to fall to somewhere around 0.75 - noticeably more than the monocyclic versions cost. That is the point of running it as a separate test: same parent, same attachment point, monocycle versus bicycle.

  agent step 1: content: Fourth product from this parent, and the systematic sweep of hinge heteroaryls continues: I have placed a pyrimidin-2-ylamine, a 2-aminopyrimidin-4-ylamine and a 4-aminoquinazoline at this same position.

This time, 4-amino-1H-pyrazolo[3,4-d]pyrimidine. The reason to try it is that it is an adenine mimic - it reproduces the hydrogen-bonding face of ATP's purine directly, which is why it underpins classic ATP-competitive kinase chemical probes. It offers the 4-amino NH and N3 acceptor like the quinazoline, but adds the pyrazole NH, giving three hydrogen-bonding points across the ring face instead of two, and it is a smaller bicycle than quinazoline so the mass penalty should be lighter than the 0.12 that one cost.

  agent step 1: content: Champion scaffold again. Last time I made a minimal change here - a methyl on the pyrimidine - which cost under 0.01. This time I will test the opposite end of the range: a full biaryl extension.

4-aryl-2-aminopyrimidine is one of the most heavily used architectures in kinase inhibitors. The aminopyrimidine binds the hinge while the 4-aryl projects into the adjacent hydrophobic pocket, and that second ring is usually where potency comes from rather than from the hinge contact alone. This molecule has the aminopyrimidine but leaves the 4-position bare, so the pocket is unoccupied.

Adding a phenyl at atom 16 takes MW from 325 to about 401 and I expect QED to fall to roughly 0.72, so this costs around 0.2 of observable Phi - by far the most expensive edit I have made on this scaffold. It is worth one oracle call because it tests whether this oracle rewards pocket occupancy on top of a hinge motif, which is the difference between the 0.10 JNK3 I have reached and the 0.2-plus the objective evidently allows.

  agent step 2: content: The nitro removal delivered exactly as before: QED 0.601 to 0.884, SA 2.09 to 1.77, MW 302. Now the second half.

The molecule has an amide NH and a pyridine, but they cannot work together - a CH2 at atom 6 separates them, so the NH and the ring nitrogen sit on opposite sides of a flexible linker instead of presenting a rigid donor-acceptor pair. That linker is also carrying two of the six rotatable bonds.

Cutting anchor 7 (the amide NH) / branch 6 discards the CH2 and the methoxypyridine together, and attaching 2-aminopyrimidin-4-yl in their place gives an N-heteroaryl amide: amide NH, pyrimidine ring nitrogen and exocyclic NH2 in the donor-acceptor-donor arrangement, directly conjugated to the carbonyl rather than held off at the end of a methylene.

MW lands near 274 and rotatable bonds drop, so this should be cheap - the analogous edit on the bromofuran scaffold cost only 0.03.

  agent step 1: content: This is a diaryl urea with QED 0.888 and SA 1.51 - excellent on both observable terms - and JNK3 of essentially zero. It is the clearest remaining case of a molecule that is well made but binds nothing.

The urea itself is worth keeping: ureas flanked by heteroaromatics are a documented JNK3 chemotype, the lineage that ran from pyrazole-ureas to thiophene-indazoles. What is wrong is that one side of this urea terminates in a bare phenyl, contributing no hydrogen-bonding partner.

Replacing that phenyl with 4-phenylpyrimidin-2-yl puts the complete architecture on the far side of the urea: a ring nitrogen adjacent to the urea NH for the hinge pair, plus a phenyl at the pyrimidine 4-position for the pocket. The urea NH-C(=O)-NH then sits where the amide linker sits in the versions that have worked.

Expect MW near 378, four aromatic rings and QED around 0.60, so roughly 0.25 of observable cost - the standard price for this motif on a scaffold with no polar group to absorb it.

  agent step 1: content: This molecule has the highest QED in the population at 0.938 and JNK3 of zero. I built it earlier in the run on the hypothesis that an isonicotinamide would engage the hinge; that hypothesis was wrong, and the pyridine nitrogen sits para to the carbonyl where it cannot pair with the amide NH.

The fix is to move the ring nitrogen adjacent to the carbonyl and add an exocyclic amine. Replacing pyridin-4-yl with 2-aminopyrimidin-4-yl gives a 2-aminopyrimidine-4-carboxamide: ring nitrogen ortho to the carbonyl, amide NH, and a 2-amino group, which is the donor-acceptor-donor face that has worked repeatedly this run.

I am deliberately taking the cheap version rather than adding a pocket aryl as well. This molecule's value is its exceptional QED, and the full 4-aryl motif costs 0.2 or more; the plain aminopyrimidine has cost as little as 0.03 elsewhere. Starting from 0.938 there is room to add binding capability and still finish with a high observable score, which is a combination nothing in the population currently has.

  agent step 1: content: Four unsubstituted rings have now been placed at this pocket position on this core - phenyl, pyridyl, thiophene, furan - so the remaining question is not which ring but whether substituting the ring helps.

4-fluorophenyl tests exactly that. It is the phenyl that produced the best molecule of the run, with a fluorine at the para position where it points deepest into the hydrophobic pocket. Para-fluorophenyl is one of the most common motifs in kinase inhibitors precisely because that position tolerates and often rewards a small electron-withdrawing substituent, and fluorine adds only 18 Da so the mass penalty is slight compared with the ring itself.

This is also the cheapest way to probe the far end of the pocket: every other extension I could make would add a whole second ring and cost 0.2 or more on top of what the first already costs.

  agent step 1: content: Same parent and same position as my previous edit, where I put a fluorine on the pocket phenyl's para carbon for 0.027 of observable cost. Chlorine is the natural companion experiment: same position, same electron-withdrawing direction, but substantially larger and more lipophilic.

That size difference is the point. If the deep pocket has room, a chlorine should fill it better than a fluorine and the oracle should reward it more; if the pocket is tight, the fluorine wins. Halogen scans at a single position are one of the standard ways this question gets answered in practice, and with the fluorine version already scored I get the comparison for the price of one more oracle call.

Cost should be modest but larger than the fluorine's - about 35 Da and roughly 0.6 of LogP, taking MW to about 401 and LogP near 3.2, so perhaps 0.04 of QED.

  agent step 1: content: Self-paired again, so mutation only. This is the unsubstituted-benzenesulfonyl version of the best scaffold, and I have now probed its pocket phenyl para position with hydrogen, fluorine and chlorine - all either neutral or electron-withdrawing.

Methoxy is the missing case and the informative one. It is electron-donating, roughly the size of a chlorine, and polar rather than lipophilic. Against the halogens it separates three explanations for whatever they did: if only the halogens help, the effect is electronic; if methoxy helps comparably, it is steric occupancy; if none of them matter, the pocket ring's substitution is irrelevant and only the ring itself counts.

It should also be relatively cheap here - it adds 30 Da and about 9 of TPSA while trimming LogP slightly from 2.63, and this molecule has more polarity headroom than the chlorinated versions did.

  agent step 1: content: This molecule carries the full architecture on a urea linker and, unlike the smaller urea variant I built earlier, it is evidently registering on the binding term - its score implies a JNK3 contribution well above that one's. So here the motif is paying and I should protect it rather than trade it away.

That points at the dimethoxyphenyl instead. Two adjacent methoxy groups on one ring is redundant: they contribute 6 of the molecule's rotatable bonds and 85 of TPSA between them while adding no binding feature, and QED is only 0.699 largely because of that flexibility and mass.

Removing one takes MW from 364 to about 334, drops a rotatable bond and roughly 9 of TPSA, and leaves the aminopyrimidine, the pocket phenyl and the urea completely untouched.

Atom 1 is the methoxy oxygen and atom 2 the aromatic carbon bearing it, so cutting anchor 2 / branch 1 discards the whole OMe cleanly.
